乙型肝炎有哪些影响?为什么应该接种疫苗?胃肠病学家 Ayush Dhingra 博士解释说健康 - 印度斯坦时报

What are the effects of Hepatitis B, and why should one vaccinate? Gastroenterologist Dr Ayush Dhingra explains | Health - Hindustan Times
新闻简报本文由《印度斯坦时报》于 2026 年 7 月 22 日发表,提供了有关 乙型肝炎病毒 (HBV) 及其对肝脏影响的一般信息。它解释说,乙型肝炎病毒可引起慢性疾病、肝硬化、肝功能衰竭和肝癌,通常在没有明显症状的情况下悄然进展。胃肠病学家 Ayush Dhingra 博士强调,疫苗接种 是预防感染和降低长期风险的最佳方法。文章指出,延迟 疫苗接种 可能会使个人容易受到终生健康后果的影响,特别是对于出生时感染的新生儿,他们面临着患慢性乙型肝炎的高风险。它没有报告任何具体的 暴发 或公共卫生应对措施,但强调了意识和及时 疫苗接种 的重要性。该内容仅供参考,不能代替专业医疗建议。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-22。该文章讨论了乙型肝炎的影响和 疫苗接种 的重要性,但没有提供具体的事件日期。
地点与对象
这篇文章在印度发表,重点关注普通人群和有乙型肝炎感染风险的个人,包括新生儿和成人。
事件
本文提供了有关乙型肝炎、其对肝脏的影响以及 疫苗接种 重要性的一般信息。这不是 暴发 或特定事件的报告,而是 Ayush Dhingra 博士的解释性文章。
规模、影响与风险
乙型肝炎可导致慢性疾病、肝硬化、肝功能衰竭和肝癌。它可能会在没有症状的情况下悄然进展,在 诊断 之前造成严重的肝损伤。延迟 疫苗接种 会增加造成终生后果的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
文章强调了 疫苗接种 作为预防措施的重要性,并提到了及时采取行动的必要性。它不报告任何具体的调查、测试或公共卫生应对措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

What are the effects of Hepatitis B, and why should one vaccinate? Gastroenterologist Dr Ayush Dhingra explains | Health - Hindustan Times

News Brief
This article, published on July 22, 2026, by Hindustan Times, provides general information about Hepatitis B virus (HBV) and its effects on the liver. It explains that HBV can cause chronic disorders, liver cirrhosis, liver failure, and liver cancer, often progressing silently without noticeable symptoms. Gastroenterologist Dr Ayush Dhingra emphasizes that vaccination is the best way to prevent infection and reduce long-term risks. The article notes that delaying vaccination can leave individuals vulnerable to lifelong health consequences, particularly for newborns infected during birth, who face a high risk of developing chronic HBV. It does not report on any specific outbreak or public health response but highlights the importance of awareness and timely vaccination. The content is intended for informational purposes and not as a substitute for professional medical advice.
Original source text (English)
Home Next What are the effects of Hepatitis B, and why should one vaccinate? Gastroenterologist Dr Ayush Dhingra explains Hepatitis B infects the liver and can cause chronic disorders in the organ over time. According to Dr Dhingra, vaccination is the best way to stay safe. Updated on: Jul 22, 2026, 18:31:13 IST By Debapriya Bhattacharya Prefer HT on Google Share via Copy link Hepatitis B is a viral infection that targets the liver, progressing silently before symptoms become noticeable. By the time many people realise something is wrong, liver damage has already taken place. Hepatitis B often evades early detection, shares Dr Ayush Dhingra. (Pexel) Also Read | Do mental workouts matter as much as physical exercise? Neurosurgeon Dr Anurag Saxena explains In an interaction with HT Lifestyle, Dr Ayush Dhingra reassured that Hepatitis B is one of the most preventable viral infections , making timely vaccination the smartest health decision a family can take. While our busy lifestyles can make it difficult to find time for vaccination, not taking the vaccine at all can come at a very high cost. What are the effects of Hepatitis B According to Dr Dhingra, there is yet to be widespread awareness about the effects of Hepatitis B. Unlike illnesses that announce their arrival with fever or cough, Hepatitis B quietly remains in the body for years. “Standard blood tests may appear normal even when the virus is causing underlying liver damage,” shared the gastroenterologist. “By the time symptoms like fatigue, jaundice, abdominal pain, or distension appear, the liver has already been damaged severely.” Some people naturally recover from the disease. However, those who do not develop a chronic infection are at increased risk of liver cirrhosis, liver failure and liver cancer. “Since infected people may not know they carry the virus, they unknowingly pass it on through infected blood, from mother to baby during childbirth or through unsafe exposure of body fluids,” noted Dr Dhingra. The importance of vaccination against Hepatitis B Vaccination is the best way to protect oneself from Hepatitis B. (Pexel) Dr Dhingra believes that prevention is the best defence against Hepatitis B. By delaying vaccination, a person leaves themselves vulnerable to an infection that has lifelong consequences like cirrhosis, liver cancer (hepatocellular carcinoma) and other conditions like glomerulonephritis, dermatologic, and rheumatologic. “Advanced liver disease demands prolonged treatment and major lifestyle changes. In severe cases where the liver loses its ability to function, liver transplantation becomes the only life-saving treatment that helps to improve quality of life,” warned the gastroenterologist. Delaying Hepatitis B vaccination leaves individuals vulnerable to infection during the period they remain unprotected. This is particularly concerning for newborns, as babies infected during birth have a very high risk of developing chronic Hepatitis B, which can lead to lifelong liver damage. Similarly, adults who postpone vaccination due to lack of awareness or misconceptions may unknowingly remain at risk through routine medical procedures, occupational exposure, or close household contact with an infected person. “Since Hepatitis B often progresses silently without noticeable symptoms, many people are diagnosed only after significant liver damage has already occurred. Timely vaccination not only prevents infection but also reduces the long-term risk of cirrhosis, liver failure, and liver cancer,” stated Dr Dhingra. Note to readers: This article is for informational purposes only and not a substitute for professional medical advice. Always seek the advice of your doctor with any questions about a medical condition. Dr Ayush Dhingra , MBBS, MD, DNB, is a consultant in the department of Gastroenterology at Manipal Hospital, Gurugram. 隐匿性乙型肝炎 突变 通过 Akt mTOR 通路驱动肝癌生长 - 新闻-医疗

Occult hepatitis B mutations drive liver cancer growth via Akt mTOR pathway - News-Medical
新闻简报发表在《基因与疾病》杂志上的一项新研究揭示了神秘的 乙型肝炎病毒 (OBI) pre-S 突变 如何通过激活 Akt/mTOR 信号通路来驱动肝癌生长。国家老年学中心和北京协和医学院的研究人员发现了加速人肝癌细胞细胞周期转变和增殖的特定突变(E39K、D44N、N98T、H128R、I161T)。这些 突变 上调大型乙型肝炎表面蛋白 (LHB),触发 Akt/mTOR 级联并增加 Cyclin D1 和相关激酶。值得注意的是,靶向激酶抑制剂 MK2206 和雷帕霉素成功抑制了这种过度增殖。虽然这些发现为精准肝细胞癌 治疗 提供了一种有前途的策略,但还需要进行更多的临床研究来将这些干预措施转化为人类疗法。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-23。证据中未明确报告事件日期。
地点与对象
研究由国家老年医学中心、北京市检验医学工程研究中心和中国协和医学院共同完成。研究使用人肝癌 (Huh-7) 细胞。
事件
发表在《基因与疾病》上的研究调查了神秘的 乙型肝炎病毒 (OBI) pre-S 突变 如何通过 Akt/mTOR 通路驱动肝癌生长。该研究确定了加速细胞周期转变和增殖的特定 突变(E39K、D44N、N98T、H128R、I161T)。
规模、影响与风险
研究表明,OBI 相关的 突变 通过上调大乙型肝炎表面蛋白 (LHB) 并激活 Akt/mTOR 信号级联,从而增加 Cyclin D1 和相关激酶,从而导致侵袭性细胞增殖。没有报告具体病例数或死亡人数。
应对、状态与不确定性
研究人员测试了靶向激酶抑制剂(MK2206 和雷帕霉素),成功抑制了 突变 驱动的过度增殖。该研究指出,需要进行更多的临床研究才能将这些有针对性的干预措施转化为人类疗法。该研究为对抗 OBI 相关肝癌提供了新策略。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Occult hepatitis B mutations drive liver cancer growth via Akt mTOR pathway - News-Medical

News Brief
A new study published in Genes & Diseases reveals how occult hepatitis B virus (OBI) pre-S mutations drive liver cancer growth by activating the Akt/mTOR signaling pathway. Researchers from National Center of Gerontology and Peking Union Medical College identified specific mutations (E39K, D44N, N98T, H128R, I161T) that accelerate cell cycle transition and proliferation in human hepatoma cells. These mutations up-regulate large hepatitis B surface protein (LHBs), triggering the Akt/mTOR cascade and increasing Cyclin D1 and associated kinases. Remarkably, targeted kinase inhibitors MK2206 and rapamycin successfully suppressed this hyper-proliferation. While the findings offer a promising strategy for precision hepatocellular carcinoma treatments, additional clinical studies are needed to translate these interventions into human therapies.
Original source text (English)
Occult hepatitis B virus (OBI) infection presents a significant global health challenge, notoriously flying under the radar of standard viral screening while relentlessly driving the pathogenesis of hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC). A critical but poorly understood factor in OBI-related tumorigenesis is the frequent emergence of mutations in the HBV pre-S genomic region. While these mutations are known to correlate with advanced liver disease, the precise molecular mechanisms by which they hijack host cellular machinery to fuel unregulated tumor growth have remained largely elusive. This new research, published in Genes & Diseases by researchers from National Center of Gerontology, Beijing Engineering Research Center of Laboratory Medicine and Peking Union Medical College, investigated the oncogenic capacity of specific OBI-associated pre-S mutations to uncover their role in aberrant cell proliferation. Through rigorous in vitro molecular experiments utilizing constructed wild-type and mutated full-length HBV plasmids transfected into human hepatoma (Huh-7) cells, the researchers systematically evaluated cellular growth and cycle dynamics. The data revealed that specific OBI-associated pre-S mutations - namely E39K, D44N, N98T, H128R, and I161T - accelerate the G1/S phase transition of the cell cycle, leading to aggressive cellular proliferation. Comprehensive protein analyses deciphered the underlying intracellular networks, demonstrating that these mutations aggressively up-regulate the expression of the large hepatitis B surface protein (LHBs). This excessive LHBs subsequently triggers a massive activation of the Akt/mTOR signaling cascade, which strongly up-regulates the cell cycle driver Cyclin D1 and its associated kinases CDK4 and CDK6. This targeted protein surge dismantles normal cellular checkpoints, unleashing uncontrolled cell division. To directly counter these severe hyper-proliferative effects, the researchers explored the therapeutic potential of targeted kinase inhibitors. Remarkably, advanced cellular assays confirmed that administering the highly specific Akt inhibitor MK2206 or the mTOR inhibitor rapamycin successfully intercepted this malignant cascade, decisively suppressing the mutation -driven hyper-proliferation and arresting the cells back in the G0/G1 phase. By chemically blocking the Akt/mTOR signaling axis, these targeted therapies stripped the mutated hepatoma cells of their oncogenic growth advantage, fundamentally shielding the liver cells from unchecked replication. While these comprehensive data robustly highlight the critical influence of OBI-associated pre-S mutations in regulating the cell cycle, additional clinical studies are necessary to translate these targeted interventions into human therapies. In conclusion, elucidating the oncogenic role of the LHBs-Akt/mTOR/Cyclin D1 signaling network offers a powerful new strategy to combat OBI-related liver cancer. This significant finding directly positions specific Akt and mTOR inhibitors as highly compelling therapeutic candidates for the next generation of precision hepatocellular carcinoma treatments. Source: Journal reference: Sun, H., et al. (2025). Potential oncogenic role of occult hepatitis B virus pre-S mutations: Activation of Akt/mTOR/Cyclin D1 signaling drives cell cycle dysregulation and proliferation in hepatocellular carcinogenesis. Genes & Diseases. DOI: 10.1016/j.gendis.2025.101919. https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2352304225004088?via%3Dihub
View five news elements

Published on 2026-07-23. Event date not explicitly reported in the evidence.

Research conducted by National Center of Gerontology, Beijing Engineering Research Center of Laboratory Medicine and Peking Union Medical College. Study used human hepatoma (Huh-7) cells.
Event
Research published in Genes & Diseases investigating how occult hepatitis B virus (OBI) pre-S mutations drive liver cancer growth via the Akt/mTOR pathway. The study identified specific mutations (E39K, D44N, N98T, H128R, I161T) that accelerate cell cycle transition and proliferation.
Scale, impact and risk
The study demonstrated that OBI-associated mutations lead to aggressive cellular proliferation by up-regulating large hepatitis B surface protein (LHBs) and activating the Akt/mTOR signaling cascade, which increases Cyclin D1 and associated kinases. No specific case counts or death counts were reported.
Response, status and uncertainty
Researchers tested targeted kinase inhibitors (MK2206 and rapamycin) that successfully suppressed mutation-driven hyper-proliferation. The study notes that additional clinical studies are necessary to translate these targeted interventions into human therapies. The research provides a new strategy for combating OBI-related liver cancer.

奥什地区超过 1,200 人登记患有乙型和丙型肝炎 - 24.KG

More than 1,200 people registered with hepatitis B and C in Osh, Osh region - 24.KG
新闻简报根据奥什地区血源性感染控制中心的数据,在吉尔吉斯斯坦奥什市和奥什地区,有 1,204 人登记患有乙型和丙型病毒性肝炎。其中,785 人患有乙型肝炎(奥什地区 609 人,奥什市 176 人),丙型肝炎 419 人(奥什地区 317 人,奥什市 102 人)。该中心强调,94%的登记病例是慢性病或有并发症,包括肝硬化和癌症,其中许多病例是在晚期才发现的。医学专家强调及时筛查、乙型肝炎 疫苗接种 和早期检测的重要性,以减少严重的健康风险。没有详细说明具体的应对措施或调查情况。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

More than 1,200 people registered with hepatitis B and C in Osh, Osh region - 24.KG

News Brief
In Osh city and Osh region, Kyrgyzstan, 1,204 people are registered with viral hepatitis B and C, according to the Osh Regional Center for the Control of Blood-Borne Infections. Of these, 785 have hepatitis B (609 in Osh region, 176 in Osh city) and 419 have hepatitis C (317 in Osh region, 102 in Osh city). The center highlights that 94% of registered cases are chronic or have complications, including liver cirrhosis and cancer, with many cases detected at later stages. Medical specialists stress the importance of timely screening, hepatitis B vaccination, and early detection to reduce severe health risks. No specific response measures or investigation status are detailed.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

WHO strengthens medical professionals’ capacity in Turkmenistan on viral hepatitis B - Turkmenportal.com

News Brief
The World Health Organization (WHO) Country Office in Turkmenistan conducted a training course on March 12-13, 2025, focused on the diagnostics and treatment of viral hepatitis B. Led by WHO/Europe consultant Professor Alexander Nersesov, the course aimed to enhance the capacity of Turkmen medical professionals in combating viral hepatitis B. Held at the Infectious disease treatment-prevention center in videoconference mode, the training covered essential information on hepatitis B diagnosis, classification, and treatment, with specific guidance for chronic cases, pregnancy, special patient groups, and hepatitis D. This initiative aligns with WHO guidelines and national protocols, equipping healthcare providers with updated knowledge to improve patient outcomes and strengthen Turkmenistan's efforts to control and manage viral hepatitis.
Original source text (English)

The course led by WHO/Europe consultant, Professor Alexander Nersesov, aims to enhance the capacity of Turkmen medical professionals in their effort to combat viral hepatitis B. The skill-building session was conducted at the Infectious disease treatment-prevention center in videoconference mode. The participants of the event were doctors specializing in the diagnosis and treatment of viral hepatitis from the Infectious disease treatment-prevention center of the Directorate of Infectious Diseases Centers, primary health care providers, infectious diseases specialists from the institutions at the district level, and representatives from the department of infectious diseases at the State Medical University named after Myrat Karryev. By enhancing the skills of healthcare providers, this initiative will contribute to improved patient outcomes and strengthen Turkmenistan's efforts to control and manage viral hepatitis. 新闻简报英国医疗保健专业人员 Tung Thanh Tran 于 2025 年 9 月在皇家自由医院去世,原因是 2013 年肾移植后乙型肝炎再激活引起的急性肝病。他于 2025 年 8 月入院,患有急性肝病迹象,他的死亡引起了人们对缺乏乙型肝炎再激活预防监测和处方的国家指导的担忧。该案例还凸显了对通过急诊科筛查确定的患者进行专门调试而没有后续参与服务的问题。这一事件凸显了英国医疗保健系统在乙型肝炎管理方面的严重差距。

时间
Published on 2026-07-21. Event occurred in August-September 2025.
地点与对象
Tung Thanh Tran,一名 41 岁的患者,有 2013 年肾移植史。
事件
Tung Thanh Tran 因肾移植后乙型肝炎重新激活引起的急性肝病死亡。该病例引起了人们对缺乏乙型肝炎再激活预防监测和处方的国家指导的担忧。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

National monitoring and prescribing guidance on hepatitis B reactivation is lacking - Pharmacy Magazine

News Brief
A UK healthcare professional, Tung Thanh Tran, died in September 2025 at the Royal Free Hospital due to acute liver disease from hepatitis B reactivation, which occurred after a renal transplant in 2013. Admitted in August 2025 with signs of acute liver disease, his death raised concerns about the lack of national guidance on monitoring and prescribing for hepatitis B reactivation prevention. The case also highlighted issues with specialized commissioning for patients identified through emergency department screening, with no subsequent engagement services. This incident underscores a critical gap in the UK's healthcare system regarding hepatitis B management.

Visit Helpdesk You recently searched for Popular Searches You recently searched for Popular Searches National monitoring and prescribing guidance on hepatitis B reactivation is lacking Neil Trainis, 21 Jul 20262 Min National monitoring and prescribing guidance on hepatitis B reactivation is lacking Neil Trainis, 21 Jul 20262 Min In News Follow this topic Bookmark Record learning outcomes A coroner in North London has called for more national guidance on which services should be responsible for monitoring and prescribing medication to prevent hepatitis B becoming active again after the death of a patient whose treatment was inadvertently discontinued. Tung Thanh Tran, 41, died at the Royal Free Hospital in September 2025 after being diagnosed with chronic hepatitis B following a renal transplant in 2013. An inquest into his death heard he was discharged from hepatology but continued to receive the viral reactivation prophylaxis entecavir from renal transplant services. By the start of 2025, the medication was being delivered to his home but was then “inadvertently discontinued as there was a presumption that hepatology would continue to prescribe” it. In his report, sent to the British Association for the Study of the Liver (BASL) and the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA), the coroner for Inner North London Richard Brittain said Tran appeared “to have understood this to have been an intentional change of his medication”. Tran was admitted to his local hospital in August 2025 with signs of acute liver disease from reactivation of hepatitis B and transferred to the Royal Free Hospital, where he died on September 12, 2025. “He was too unwell to be considered for liver transplant,” Brittain noted in his report. He recorded the cause of death as acute liver failure, sepsis (unknown source), hepatitis B reactivation owing to cessation of medication and immunosuppression for renal transplantation. “Mr Tran died of complications arising from reactivation of a viral illness, which arose from inadvertent discontinuation of necessary medical treatment,” the report concluded. Brittain told BASL that Tran’s death gave rise to concerns about “a lack of national guidance regarding which services should be responsible for the monitoring and prescribing in relation to hepatitis B reactivation prevention”. Brittain also told the UKHSA: “There is a large population of patients who are found to have hepatitis B infection through opt-out screening in emergency departments but there is a lack of specialised commissioning to maintain subsequent engagement with services. “I heard that this differs from the position with regards to hepatitis C and HIV services, even though these patient populations are smaller.” The report was also sent to Tran’s family and the Royal Free Hospital. _ https://www.pharmacymagazine.co.uk/news/national-monitoring-and-prescribing-guidance-on-hepatitis-b-reactivation-is-lacking Copy Link Record my learning outcomes Save for later News Clinical #Hepatitis #Liver #Prescription medicines #Coroner Share: In News Stay up to date with all the news, learning and insight in the world of pharmacy. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Coroner calls for more guidance on hepatitis B reactivation prescribing - Chemist+Druggist

News Brief
A coroner has called for improved national guidance on hepatitis B reactivation prescribing after a patient died following the inadvertent discontinuation of necessary medication. Tung Tran, 41, died in September 2025 from complications of hepatitis B reactivation caused by the cessation of Entecavir, which he had been receiving since a 2013 renal transplant. The assistant coroner for Inner London North highlighted a 'lack of national guidance' on monitoring and prescribing for hepatitis B reactivation, warning of a 'significant risk of future deaths.' The coroner directed concerns to the British Viral Hepatitis group and the UK Health Security Agency, noting variation in NHS practices and gaps in specialized commissioning for hepatitis B patients identified through emergency screenings. The British Association for the Study of the Liver acknowledged the issue but stated it does not develop clinical guidelines, instead planning to incorporate the risks into educational programs.
Original source text (English)
A coroner’s report published after a patient died of complications from a hepatitis B reactivation warned that there is “significant risk of future deaths”. A “lack of national guidance” on monitoring and prescribing for hepatitis B reactivation poses a “significant risk of future deaths”, a coroner has warned. In a prevention of future deaths report published earlier this month, assistant coroner for Inner London North Richard Brittain noted that Tung Tran, 41, died in September 2025 of complications from a hepatitis B reactivation caused by “inadvertent discontinuation of necessary medical treatment”. The report said Tran was diagnosed with hepatitis B after a renal transplant in 2013 and, “after a period of disengagement from services”, was “discharged from hepatology but continued to receive viral reactivation prophylaxis, Entecavir, from renal transplant services”. It added that Tran’s medication supply changed to home delivery in early 2025, but Entecavir was “inadvertently discontinued”, with the “presumption that hepatology would continue to prescribe this”. Tran “appears to have understood this to have been an intentional change of his medication”, Brittain said. Read more: Coroner warns of prescribing errors after prison death “He attended his local hospital in August 2025 with signs of acute liver disease from reactivation of hepatitis B and was transferred to the Royal Free Hospital,” he added. “He was too unwell to be considered for liver transplant and sadly died on September 12, 2025′′. According to the report, an inquest into Tran’s death found the medical cause to be acute liver failure, sepsis, hepatitis B reactivation “owing to cessation of medication” and immunosuppression for renal transplantation. Brittain highlighted to the British Viral Hepatitis group at the British Association for the Study of the Liver (BASL) a concern that “there is a lack of national guidance regarding which services should be responsible for monitoring and prescribing in relation to hepatitis B reactivation prevention”. He also directed a concern to the UK Health Security Agency, noting that although a “large population” of patients are found to have hepatitis B through opt-out screenings at emergency departments, “there is a lack of specialised commissioning to maintain subsequent engagement with services”. And he said that “unless action is taken to address” the concerns, there is “a significant risk of future deaths”. Read more: ‘Highly toxic’: Coroner warns GPs of propranolol overdose risk In its response, the BASL said it “does not lead the development or publication of clinical guidelines”. It added that there “is variation between NHS Trusts” as to whether monitoring and prescribing for patients at risk of hepatitis B reactivation in the setting of immunosuppressive treatment is “delivered by the treating team or whether specialist hepatology services are involved”. “We have previously and will again incorporate the risks of hepatitis B reactivation and its prevention and management into our webinar and conference programmes in this coming year,” it said. Read more: Coroner warns of issues with epilepsy meds availability It comes after a coroner raised concerns earlier this year over prescribing errors at HMP Thameside following the death of an inmate. And it follows a coroner warning GPs of the risk of propranolol overdose after the death of a man who took a “significant amount” of the drug. Declan joined C+D as a reporter in July 2026. He has previous bylines in The Drinks Business magazine and graduated with an MA in Journalism from City St George's, University of London with Distinction earlier this year. The regulator has updated product information for the drug to include a contraindication for patients with phaeochromocytoma. The analysis also revealed that 67% of Pharmacy First users were female and 59% were aged below 40. The GPhC said the pharmacist was working in an unregulated aesthetic clinic when she prescribed Mounjaro to patients without the appropriate clinical checks

CT 测量的脂肪和肌肉变化可预测 慢性乙型肝炎 相关肝硬化的严重肝脏并发症新闻明智 - 新闻明智

CT-Measured Fat and Muscle Changes Predict Severe Liver Complications in Chronic Hepatitis B-Related Cirrhosis | Newswise - Newswise
新闻简报中国研究人员发表在《肝胆通讯》上的一项新研究表明,CT 测量的脂肪和肌肉变化可以预测 慢性乙型肝炎 相关肝硬化患者的严重肝脏并发症。研究发现,对于早期肝硬化患者来说,内脏脂肪逐渐退化预示着更高的并发症风险,而对于那些患有严重失代偿事件的患者来说,肌肉萎缩是主要的警告标志。这些组织模式可以通过常规腹部 CT 扫描检测到,使临床医生能够提供早期个性化的营养和代谢支持,有可能减缓肝损伤并提高长期生存率。该研究解决了目前依赖一次性肝脏血液测试的临床实践中的一个关键差距,这种测试错过了导致疾病恶化的逐渐的全身组织变化。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表日期:2026-07-20。事件日期:未在提供的证据中明确报告。
地点与对象
国家:中国(由中国科学院和兰州大学暗示)。人群:慢性乙型肝炎 相关肝硬化患者。
事件
发表在 Hepatobiliary Communications 上的研究发现,CT 测量的脂肪和肌肉变化可以预测 慢性乙型肝炎 相关肝硬化患者的严重肝脏并发症。该研究从标准 CT 扫描中确定了特定阶段的组织模式,以预测即将出现的并发症。
规模、影响与风险
病例:未见报道。死亡人数:未报告。风险:对于代偿性早期肝硬化,内脏脂肪的逐渐退化预示着并发症的风险更高。对于患有严重肝代偿失调事件的患者,持续性肌肉萎缩是主要的警告标志。
应对、状态与不确定性
调查:在同行评审期刊上发表的已完成研究。测试:使用常规腹部 CT 扫描,无需额外测试。预防:允许临床医生尽早启动个性化的营养和代谢支持。现状:研究结果解决了日常肝病管理中未满足的临床需求。不确定性:所提供的证据中未报告。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

CT-Measured Fat and Muscle Changes Predict Severe Liver Complications in Chronic Hepatitis B-Related Cirrhosis | Newswise - Newswise

News Brief
A new study from Chinese researchers, published in Hepatobiliary Communications, reveals that CT-measured changes in fat and muscle can predict severe liver complications in chronic hepatitis B-related cirrhosis patients. The study found that for patients with early-stage cirrhosis, gradual visceral fat degeneration signals higher complication risks, while for those with severe decompensation events, muscle wasting is the primary warning marker. These tissue patterns can be detected on routine abdominal CT scans, allowing clinicians to provide early personalized nutritional and metabolic support, potentially slowing liver damage and improving long-term survival. The research addresses a critical gap in current clinical practices that rely on one-time liver blood tests, which miss gradual whole-body tissue changes driving disease deterioration.
Original source text (English)

Newswise — Each year, millions of people live with chronic hepatitis B that slowly develops into cirrhosis. Once cirrhosis patients suffer decompensation issues including fluid buildup in the belly and dangerous esophageal bleeding, their risks of repeated hospital stays and early death rise sharply.Clinicians mostly judge liver risk through one-time liver blood tests, which miss gradual whole-body tissue changes that drive disease deterioration. In a study published in Hepatobiliary Communications, researchers from The First Hospital of Lanzhou University uncovered clear, stage-specific tissue patterns from standard CT scans to predict upcoming severe liver complications.“We found that visceral fat and muscle shifts carry totally different warning meaning based on how advanced a patient’s cirrhosis is,” shares co-lead author Xiaoyue Zhang. “For patients with compensated early-stage cirrhosis, gradual degeneration of visceral fat signals a significantly higher risk of complications, and for those who have already experienced severe hepatic decompensation events, persistent muscle wasting serves as the primary warning marker.”The researchers confirmed tracking long-term tissue shifts delivers much more accurate risk alerts than isolated examinations. “Above all, these fat and muscle changes can be spotted on routine abdominal CT scans that every cirrhosis patient already undergoes, with no extra expensive or invasive testing needed,” adds Zhang.According to senior and corresponding author Junfeng Li, the team’s findings addressed an unmet clinical demand in daily liver disease management. “Past research rarely split cirrhosis patients by disease stage to sort out these tissue warning signals,” explains Li. “This easy CT monitoring method allows clinicians to launch personalized nutritional and metabolic support early, effectively slowing liver damage and lifting long-term survival chances for hepatitis B cirrhosis patients.”###ReferencesDOI10.1016/j.hbc.2026.100003Original Source URLhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.hbc.2026.100003Funding InformationThis work was supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China [Grant no. 82360132]; and the Major Project of Scientific and Technological Innovation in the Health Industry of Gansu Province [Grant no. GSWSZD2024-11].About Hepatobiliary CommunicationsHepatobiliary Communications is an international, peer-reviewed, open-access journal dedicated to advancing the prevention, diagnosis, treatment, and translational understanding of liver and biliary diseases. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

ETSU 研究人员寻求乙型肝炎的治疗方法 - Kingsport Times News

ETSU researcher seeks cure for Hepatitis B - Kingsport Times News
新闻简报A news report from the Kingsport Times News highlights the work of East Tennessee State University (ETSU) researcher Dr. Yao, who is leading the nation's only National Institutes of Health (NIH)-funded R33 project aimed at developing a cure for Hepatitis B. The project, which received a $1.76 million grant from the NIH’s Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases (NIAID), is in its second phase of research and focuses on gene therapy and CRISPR-based gene-editing approaches to eliminate the virus from infected cells. Dr. Yao's team has developed an exosome-based delivery system designed to target liver cells. The research is part of ETSU's growing commitment to health innovation, with the potential to benefit patients in Appalachia and beyond. The findings are based on target-specific methods and results, and the progress is described as a potential curative strategy for Hepatitis B. No specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are reported in the evidence.
查看新闻五要素
时间
自定义您的 cookie 偏好 我们尊重您的隐私权。
地点与对象
东田纳西州立大学(ETSU),霍金斯县,田纳西州,美国;研究小组专注于乙型肝炎病毒(HBV)感染。
事件
Yao is leading a research project to develop a cure for Hepatitis B, which is the only NIH-funded R33 project in the nation. The research involves gene therapy and CRISPR-based gene-editing approaches to eliminate the virus from infected cells. The project received a $1.76 million grant from the NIH’s Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases (NIAID) to advance to a second phase of research.
规模、影响与风险
这项研究有可能开发出从体内消除 乙型肝炎病毒 的疗法,而不是简单地控制它。该项目是 ETSU 不断发展的研究事业的一部分,其致力于通过科学发现改善健康,重点是造福阿巴拉契亚及其他地区的患者。证据中没有报告具体的病例数、死亡数或暴露人数。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究继 2024 年成功完成第一阶段 (R21) 后,目前正处于第二阶段 (R33)。该项目由 NIH 资助,涉及使用基于 CRISPR 的基因编辑技术。研究小组正在研究一种基于外泌体的递送系统,以靶向感染的肝细胞。这些发现基于针对特定目标的方法和结果,并将这一进展报告为乙型肝炎的潜在治疗策略。

Tennessee man dead after Scott County car crash News |Region |EducationETSU researcher seeks cure for Hepatitis BFrom staff reportsPublished: Jul 20, 2026 2:01 PMUpdated: Jul 21, 2026 5:08 PMFeatured MediaPhoto Credit: CONTRIBUTEDDr. Zhi Q. YaoDr. Zhi Q. YaoCONTRIBUTED568317East Tennessee State University’s Dr. Zhi Q. Yao is leading the nation’s only National Institutes of Health (NIH)-funded R33 project to advance to a second phase of research aimed at developing a cure for Hepatitis B.Yao, a professor in the Quillen College of Medicine’s Department of Internal Medicine, received the $1.76 million grant from the NIH’s Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases (NIAID) to continue developing a gene therapy approach that could eliminate the Hepatitis B infection, according to a press release from ETSU.The Phase II (R33) award follows the Phase 1 (R21) award Yao received in 2024 from the NIAID. Of the five research teams that received Phase I grants in 2024, Yao was the only one to receive Phase II funding.“This recognition reflects the progress our team has made toward developing a potential curative strategy for Hepatitis B,” Yao said. “The Phase II award allows us to build upon our earlier work with new gene-editing approaches that could ultimately eliminate the virus from infected cells.”Yao’s work focuses on improving the delivery of CRISPR-based gene editing technology. This research has led to the development of an exosome-based delivery system designed to target infected cells in the liver. Exosomes are small, cell-derived, membrane-bound structures that facilitate communication between cells by carrying proteins, lipids, RNA and other molecules.If successful, the approach could lead to therapies that eliminate the virus from the body rather than simply controlling it.“Being the only team selected to continue this work is an extraordinary accomplishment for Dr. Yao and his team,” Dr. Bill Block, Quillen’s dean and ETSU’s vice president for Clinical Affairs, said. “His award is not just a testament to his excellence as a researcher – it also speaks to the strength of our college’s research program, and our ability to impact health both inside and outside of a clinical setting.”The award reflects ETSU's growing research enterprise and its commitment to improving health through scientific discovery – advancing innovations that have the potential to benefit patients in Appalachia and beyond, according to the release. It also brings Yao’s total NIH funding in this line of research to more than $4.3 million.Yao serves as Quillen’s Paul Dishner Chair of Excellence, director of the college’s Center of Excellence for HIV/AIDS, and chief of the Department of Internal Medicine’s research division. Yao is also a member of ETSU’s Center of Excellence in Inflammation, Infectious Disease and Immunity. More information about the Quillen College of Medicine is available online at etsu.edu/com/. Location and population
East Tennessee State University (ETSU), Hawkins County, Tennessee, United States; research team focused on Hepatitis B virus (HBV) infection.
Event
Yao is leading a research project to develop a cure for Hepatitis B, which is the only NIH-funded R33 project in the nation. The research involves gene therapy and CRISPR-based gene-editing approaches to eliminate the virus from infected cells. The project received a $1.76 million grant from the NIH’s Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases (NIAID) to advance to a second phase of research.
Scale, impact and risk
The research has the potential to lead to therapies that eliminate the Hepatitis B virus from the body rather than simply controlling it. The project is part of ETSU's growing research enterprise and its commitment to improving health through scientific discovery, with a focus on benefiting patients in Appalachia and beyond. No specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are reported in the evidence.
Response, status and uncertainty
The research is in the second phase (R33) of development, following a successful first phase (R21) in 2024. The project is funded by the NIH and involves the use of CRISPR-based gene-editing technology. The research team is working on an exosome-based delivery system to target infected liver cells. The findings are based on target-specific methods and results, and the progress is reported as a potential curative strategy for Hepatitis B.

Original source text (English)
President John Dramani Mahama has expressed concern over the growing burden of diseases across Africa, saying they continue to claim lives and undermine the continent’s development. He said more than 90 million Africans living with hepatitis B or C remain undiagnosed, while malaria continues to pose a major health challenge. He also noted that non-communicable diseases account for more than two million deaths among Africans below the age of 70 each year. President Mahama made the remarks at the African Union Extraordinary Health Summit, where African Heads of State and Governments gathered to adopt a fully costed continental roadmap aimed at ending AIDS and tuberculosis by 2030. In his opening address, the President called on African leaders to intensify efforts to address the continent’s health challenges, stressing that Africa must move beyond traditional approaches to healthcare and pursue transformative solutions. “Neglected tropical diseases still blind, disfigure and stigmatise our people, and if you are observant you will notice that most accounts of the global infectious diseases burden present a uniquely depressing African perspective. “Africa now stands at a crossroad defined by its appetites for total health transformation. It can no longer allow itself to be purged on the periphery and cast as the destitute beneficiary of the largesse of some distant global health systems,” he said. President Mahama further urged leaders to ensure that commitments made at the summit translate into concrete actions and measurable outcomes rather than remain mere declarations. He said sustained political commitment, investment, and stronger health systems are essential to achieving lasting improvements in healthcare delivery across the continent. CT 测量的脂肪和肌肉变化可预测 慢性乙型肝炎 相关肝硬化的严重肝脏并发症

CT-measured fat and muscle changes predict severe liver complications in Chronic Hepatitis B-Related Cirrhosis
新闻简报兰州大学第一医院的一项开创性研究表明,常规CT扫描可以预测慢性乙型肝炎相关肝硬化患者的严重肝脏并发症。研究人员发现了不同的组织模式:早期代偿期患者的内脏脂肪恶化预示着风险,而持续的肌肉损失则警告失代偿性疾病患者存在危险。这种非侵入性监测方法使临床医生能够通过个性化营养支持进行早期干预,有可能减缓肝损伤并提高生存率。该研究解决了当前临床实践中的一个关键差距,即依赖于一次性血液测试,而错过了导致疾病进展的逐渐组织变化。全球有数百万人患有乙型肝炎相关肝硬化,这种方法为更好的疾病管理提供了一种实用、经济高效的工具。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 20 日。证据中未明确报告事件日期。
地点与对象
研究在兰州大学第一医院进行。研究人群包括 慢性乙型肝炎 相关肝硬化患者。
事件
研究人员发现,CT 测量的脂肪和肌肉变化可以预测 慢性乙型肝炎 相关肝硬化的严重肝脏并发症。该研究发现了特定阶段的组织模式:内脏脂肪变性恶化预示着代偿早期患者的并发症,而持续的肌肉损失则预示着失代偿患者的风险。
规模、影响与风险
该研究解决了肝病管理中未满足的临床需求。数百万人患有 慢性乙型肝炎,并发展为肝硬化。失代偿患者面临着反复住院和过早死亡的风险增加。研究结果允许早期个性化营养和代谢支持,以减缓肝损伤并提高长期生存率。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究发表在《肝胆通讯》上,并得到了多个资金来源的支持。该方法使用已对肝硬化患者进行的常规腹部 CT 扫描,无需额外测试。这些发现提供了一种非侵入性的方法来监测疾病进展并指导临床干预。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

CT-measured fat and muscle changes predict severe liver complications in Chronic Hepatitis B-Related Cirrhosis

News Brief
A groundbreaking study from The First Hospital of Lanzhou University reveals that routine CT scans can predict severe liver complications in chronic hepatitis B-related cirrhosis patients. Researchers discovered distinct tissue patterns: worsening visceral fat signals risk in early-stage compensated patients, while persistent muscle loss warns of danger in those with decompensated disease. This non-invasive monitoring method allows clinicians to intervene early with personalized nutritional support, potentially slowing liver damage and improving survival rates. The study addresses a critical gap in current clinical practice, which relies on one-time blood tests that miss gradual tissue changes driving disease progression. With millions living with hepatitis B-related cirrhosis globally, this approach offers a practical, cost-effective tool for better disease management.
Original source text (English)
Skip to content This page contains press release content distributed by XPR Media. Members of the editorial and news staff of the USA TODAY Network were not involved in the creation of this content. The placement of this content is part of a paid service. CT-measured fat and muscle changes predict severe liver complications in Chronic Hepatitis B-Related Cirrhosis Distributed by EIN Presswire FAYETTEVILLE, GA, UNITED STATES, July 20, 2026 /EINPresswire.com/ — Researchers tracked longitudinal alterations in adipose and muscle tissue through routine CT scans. Worsening visceral fat tissue degeneration predicts complications for compensated chronic hepatitis B-related cirrhosis, while sustained muscle loss signals risk in decompensated patients. Each year, millions of people live with chronic hepatitis B that slowly develops into cirrhosis. Once cirrhosis patients suffer decompensation issues including fluid buildup in the belly and dangerous esophageal bleeding, their risks of repeated hospital stays and early death rise sharply. Clinicians mostly judge liver risk through one-time liver blood tests, which miss gradual whole-body tissue changes that drive disease deterioration. In a study published in Hepatobiliary Communications, researchers from The First Hospital of Lanzhou University uncovered clear, stage-specific tissue patterns from standard CT scans to predict upcoming severe liver complications. “We found that visceral fat and muscle shifts carry totally different warning meaning based on how advanced a patient’s cirrhosis is,” shares co-lead author Xiaoyue Zhang. “For patients with compensated early-stage cirrhosis, gradual degeneration of visceral fat signals a significantly higher risk of complications, and for those who have already experienced severe hepatic decompensation events, persistent muscle wasting serves as the primary warning marker.” The researchers confirmed tracking long-term tissue shifts delivers much more accurate risk alerts than isolated examinations. “Above all, these fat and muscle changes can be spotted on routine abdominal CT scans that every cirrhosis patient already undergoes, with no extra expensive or invasive testing needed,” adds Zhang. According to senior and corresponding author Junfeng Li, the team’s findings addressed an unmet clinical demand in daily liver disease management. “Past research rarely split cirrhosis patients by disease stage to sort out these tissue warning signals,” explains Li. “This easy CT monitoring method allows clinicians to launch personalized nutritional and metabolic support early, effectively slowing liver damage and lifting long-term survival chances for hepatitis B cirrhosis patients.” ReferencesDOI10.1016/j.hbc.2026.100003 Original Source URLhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.hbc.2026.100003 Funding InformationThis work was supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China [Grant no. 82360132]; the National College Students Innovation and Entrepreneurship Training Program [Grant no. 20250000009]; the Gansu Province College Students Innovation and Entrepreneurship Training Plan Project [Grant no. 20250060289]; the Excellence Program of Lanzhou University [Grant no. 20240060001]; the Lanzhou University College Students Innovation and Entrepreneurship [Grant nos. 20250020069, 20250020072]; the Gansu Province Joint Research Fund Project [Grant nos. 23JRRA1489, 24JRRA911]; and the Major Project of Scientific and Technological Innovation in the Health Industry of Gansu Province [Grant no. Precision BioSciences Advances Gene-Editing Programs in Hepatitis B

News Brief
Precision BioSciences has advanced its gene-editing therapy PBGENE-HBV for chronic hepatitis B, presenting clinical data from the ELIMINATE-B study. The therapy demonstrated liver biopsy evidence of directly eliminating cccDNA and achieving a 1-log reduction in cccDNA-derived transcripts. A second clinical site at Washington University School of Medicine in St. Louis has been activated for the related FUNCTION-DMD trial. Both programs aim for initial or next data updates around year-end 2026, indicating steady progress in the company's gene-editing pipeline. The announcement comes from the company itself, with no independent review by The Globe and Mail.

肝炎预防计划可改善肝脏预后,但筛查和护理方面仍存在差距 - 美国管理护理杂志

Hepatitis Prevention Programs Improve Liver Outcomes, but Gaps in Screening, Care Remain - The American Journal of Managed Care
新闻简报《公共卫生》杂志上发表的一项系统综述表明,结合筛查、疫苗接种、早期 治疗 和长期监测的乙型肝炎和丙型肝炎病毒预防计划可显着改善高收入国家的肝脏相关结果。基于社区的筛查在 15.3-34.3% 的高危人群中发现了未确诊的感染,而综合护理在 5.3-46.2% 的参与者中发现了晚期肝病。早期的 治疗 与降低肝细胞癌风险相关,澳大利亚扩大的预防工作估计在 2015 年至 2022 年间预防了 969 例 HCC 病例和 1,427 例死亡。尽管有这些好处,但实施方面的挑战,包括分散的医疗保健、有限的资源以及耻辱和随访困难(11.5-39.6% 的随访损失)等患者障碍,继续阻碍项目的有效性。作者强调需要采取综合的、以患者为中心的方法来实现可持续和公平的肝炎预防。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表于 2026 年 7 月 19 日。系统评价评估了 2013 年 1 月至 2025 年 11 月期间发表的研究。
地点与对象
高收入国家,重点关注高风险人群,包括注射吸毒者、来自肝炎流行地区的个人以及面临护理方面的社会经济障碍的人。
事件
《公共卫生》杂志上发表的一项系统综述发现,结合筛查、疫苗接种、早期 治疗 和长期监测的乙型和丙型肝炎病毒 (HBV/HCV) 预防计划可以改善检测、治疗 摄取和肝脏相关结果。
规模、影响与风险
基于社区的筛查举措发现,15.3% 至 34.3% 的筛查个体存在未确诊的 HBV/HCV 感染。综合护理计划检测到 5.3% 至 46.2% 的参与者患有晚期纤维化,3.5% 至 39.2% 的参与者患有肝癌。早期的 诊断 和 治疗 与改善长期肝脏结果相关。在澳大利亚,2015 年至 2022 年间扩大的预防策略估计可预防 969 例 HCV 相关 HCC 病例和 1,427 例 HCV 相关死亡。
应对、状态与不确定性
尽管有证据支持预防策略,但医疗保健服务分散、劳动力能力有限、减害服务不足、资金不稳定、患者数据系统不完整以及相互竞争的临床优先事项等障碍限制了计划的有效性。患者层面的障碍,如肝炎意识有限、耻辱、社会经济不稳定以及维持随访困难(失访率从 11.5% 到 39.6%)也影响了护理。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Hepatitis Prevention Programs Improve Liver Outcomes, but Gaps in Screening, Care Remain - The American Journal of Managed Care

News Brief
A systematic review published in Public Health demonstrates that hepatitis B and C virus prevention programs incorporating screening, vaccination, early treatment, and long-term monitoring significantly improve liver-related outcomes in high-income countries. Community-based screening identified undiagnosed infections in 15.3-34.3% of at-risk individuals, while integrated care detected advanced liver disease in 5.3-46.2% of participants. Earlier treatment was associated with reduced hepatocellular carcinoma risk, with Australia's expanded prevention efforts estimated to prevent 969 HCC cases and 1,427 deaths between 2015-2022. Despite these benefits, implementation challenges including fragmented healthcare, limited resources, and patient barriers like stigma and follow-up difficulties (11.5-39.6% loss to follow-up) continue to hinder program effectiveness. The authors emphasize the need for integrated, patient-centered approaches to achieve sustainable and equitable hepatitis prevention.
Original source text (English)
Hepatitis B and C virus (HBV/HCV) prevention programs incorporating screening, vaccination, early treatment, and long-term monitoring can improve detection, treatment uptake, and liver-related outcomes, according to a systematic review recently published in Public Health . 1 Assessing the Impact of HBV/HCV Prevention Programs Chronic HBV and HCV infections remain leading causes of hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) and long-term liver disease, highlighting the need for prevention strategies that extend beyond infection control. 2 Although advances such as direct-acting antivirals have transformed HCV treatment and expanded prevention efforts, gaps remain in identifying undiagnosed infections, ensuring timely treatment, and maintaining long-term liver disease monitoring. 1 In high-income countries, hepatitis prevention programs often focus on populations at increased risk, including people who inject drugs, individuals from hepatitis-endemic regions, and those facing socioeconomic barriers to care . This systematic review evaluated the effectiveness, cost-effectiveness, and implementation challenges of HBV/HCV prevention programs aimed at addressing chronic liver disease and HCC through screening, vaccination, antiviral treatment, and ongoing surveillance. Researchers searched MEDLINE, Embase, CINAHL, and Scopus for studies published between January 2013 and November 2025 evaluating HBV and/or HCV prevention programs in high-income countries. Included studies assessed strategies such as population-based screening, targeted outreach, vaccination, antiviral treatment, liver monitoring, and HCC surveillance. Early Treatment Associated With Lower HCC Risk and Reduced Costs Across 23 studies, community-based screening initiatives targeting populations at elevated risk identified undiagnosed HBV/HCV infections in 15.3% to 34.3% of screened individuals. In addition, integrated care pathways and monitoring programs enabled detection of advanced fibrosis in 5.3% to 46.2% of participants and HCC in 3.5% to 39.2%. Several successful programs used multidisciplinary models that combined primary care, specialty care, community organizations, and case management. These programs frequently incorporated point-of-care testing, noninvasive liver assessments, vaccination, antiviral therapy, and patient navigation services to improve linkage to care. The review also found that earlier diagnosis and treatment were associated with improved long-term liver outcomes. One population-based analysis included in the review found that individuals with cirrhosis had a 2.74-fold higher risk of developing HCC (adjusted sub-distribution HR [aSHR], 2.74; 95% CI, 1.78-4.22), whereas individuals who achieved sustained virologic response following HCV treatment experienced an 82% reduction in HCC risk (aSHR, 0.18; 95% CI, 0.12-0.27). Modeling studies also demonstrated potential population-level benefits from expanded prevention strategies. In Australia, expanded screening, access to antiviral treatment, and monitoring between 2015 and 2022 were estimated to prevent 969 HCV-related HCC cases and 1427 HCV-related deaths. Economic analyses consistently supported the value of prevention programs. Expanded HBV/HCV screening, early antiviral treatment, vaccination, and HCC surveillance were generally considered cost-effective, with incremental cost-effectiveness ratios ranging from $1890 to $187,000 per quality-adjusted life-year gained. Implementation Barriers Continue to Affect Hepatitis Prevention Despite evidence supporting prevention strategies, several barriers limited program effectiveness. The review identified fragmented health care delivery, limited workforce capacity, inadequate harm-reduction services, unstable funding, incomplete patient data systems, and competing clinical priorities as challenges to implementation. Patient-level barriers also affected treatment initiation and long-term monitoring. The researchers noted that limited hepatitis awareness, stigma, socioeconomic instability, and difficulties maintaining follow-up contributed to gaps in care; across studies reporting these outcomes, loss to follow-up ranged from 11.5% to 39.6%. Programs demonstrating greater success incorporated accessible and subsidized testing and treatment, coordinated care pathways, automated health record reminders, and partnerships between health systems and community organizations. Integrated Care Models May Improve Long-Term Hepatitis Outcomes Based on their review, the authors concluded that hepatitis prevention efforts must extend beyond identifying infections by incorporating comprehensive care models spanning diagnosis, treatment, and surveillance. However, they acknowledged several limitations, including that most analyzed studies had limited follow-up periods and heterogeneous contexts and outcome measures, making long-term program effects unclear and reducing generalizability. The researchers emphasized that additional longitudinal research is needed to determine whether large-scale implementation of hepatitis prevention programs directly reduces HCC incidence and mortality. “...much of the evidence is constrained by small-scale implementation, inconsistent reporting, and limited examination of behavioural and structural determinants of participation and adherence,” they wrote. “Strengthening the implementation of patient-centered, integrated system-wide approaches is needed to support sustainable and equitable prevention efforts.” References Cai S, Dharmayani PNA, Lewandowska M, et al. Impacts of hepatitis B and C prevention programmes on long-term outcomes of advanced liver disease and hepatocellular carcinoma: a systematic review. Public Health . Published online July 3, 2026. doi:10.1016/j.puhe.2026.106384 Llovet JM, Kelley RK, Villanueva A, et al. Hepatocellular carcinoma. Nat Rev Dis Primers . 2021;7(1):6. doi:10.1038/s41572-020-00240-3

Event
A systematic review published in Public Health found that hepatitis B and C virus (HBV/HCV) prevention programs incorporating screening, vaccination, early treatment, and long-term monitoring can improve detection, treatment uptake, and liver-related outcomes.
Scale, impact and risk
Community-based screening initiatives identified undiagnosed HBV/HCV infections in 15.3% to 34.3% of screened individuals. Integrated care programs detected advanced fibrosis in 5.3% to 46.2% of participants and HCC in 3.5% to 39.2%. Earlier diagnosis and treatment were associated with improved long-term liver outcomes. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Social participation essential to prevent hepatitis - Prothom Alo English

News Brief
A roundtable discussion in Dhaka highlighted Bangladesh's struggle to eliminate hepatitis by 2030, with 10 million people living with hepatitis B and C and the country among the top 10 for hepatitis-related deaths. Experts emphasized that social participation and prevention are crucial, as treatment alone is insufficient. The Hepatology Society and DGHS are prioritizing awareness, screening, and vaccine expansion, with a focus on high-risk groups including women of reproductive age, children, and job seekers. Challenges include employment discrimination against HBV-positive individuals and transmission risks from contaminated food and mother-to-child spread. The event underscored the need for science-based media coverage and nationwide campaigns to combat ignorance and stigma.
Original source text (English)
Around 10 million (1 crore) people in Bangladesh are living with hepatitis B and hepatitis C. Bangladesh is among the 10 countries with the highest number of hepatitis-related deaths worldwide. The country remains far behind in achieving the goal of eliminating hepatitis by 2030. Participants at a roundtable discussion said that achieving this target will require broad social participation. These observations emerged at a roundtable discussion titled “Hepatitis: Social Participation is essential for prevention and control”, held yesterday, Tuesday, at the Prothom Alo office in Karwan Bazar, Dhaka. The Hepatology Society of Dhaka, Bangladesh, and Prothom Alo jointly organised the event ahead of World Hepatitis Day, observed on 28 July. Speaking as the chief guest, Professor Foara Tasmim, additional director general (Planning and Development) at the Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS), said, "We cannot solve the hepatitis problem through treatment alone. We must ensure that people do not contract the disease in the first place. We need to raise public awareness, encourage people to protect themselves from infection, and ensure treatment for those who become infected." She added that the DGHS has prioritised hepatitis prevention and is discussing expanding vaccine coverage and ensuring an adequate vaccine supply with Gavi. At the beginning of the discussion, Mohammad Saiful Islam, associate professor in the Department of Liver Diseases at Bangladesh Medical University and Scientific Secretary of the Hepatology Society, cited findings from a study showing that 8.5 million (85 lakh) people in Bangladesh are infected with the hepatitis B virus. Of them, 5.7 million (57 lakh) are men and 2.8 million (28 lakh) are women. Among women of reproductive age between 18 and 45 years, around 1.8 million (18 lakh) are living with the infection, while approximately 400,000 children are affected. Nearly 1.5 million (15 lakh) families in Bangladesh have at least one member infected with the hepatitis B virus. Mohammad Saiful Islam said that around 2.5 million (25 lakh) job seekers and working-age people aged 15 to 30 years are infected with hepatitis B. He noted that many employers automatically declare hepatitis B-positive candidates ineligible for employment, adding, "This is not a scientifically justified practice." The event also highlighted that several types of hepatitis viruses exist, including hepatitis A, hepatitis B, hepatitis C, hepatitis D, and hepatitis E, each causing different forms of the disease. Hepatitis C is a leading cause of liver cirrhosis and liver cancer. Ten countries account for 69 per cent of hepatitis C-related deaths worldwide, including China, India, Bangladesh, Indonesia, Nigeria, Ethiopia, Ghana, the Philippines, South Africa, and Vietnam. Mohammad Kamrul Anam, physician at the National Institute of Gastroenterology and Liver Diseases and Hospital and International Affairs Secretary of the Hepatology Society, emphasised the importance of screening and vaccination to prevent hepatitis transmission. He said that all adults living in areas with a high prevalence of hepatitis B should undergo hepatitis testing at least once in their lifetime. He added that people with liver disease, individuals with weakened immune systems, people who use or previously used injectable drugs, prisoners, patients undergoing dialysis, blood or organ donors, healthcare workers, and pregnant women should all undergo hepatitis screening. He then explained the methods used for hepatitis screening and diagnosis. Tanvir Ahmad, assistant professor at Bangladesh Medical University and Media and Publications Secretary of the Hepatology Society, said that hepatitis is preventable, detectable, treatable and, in many cases, can be eliminated. He said that the Hepatology Society organises education and training programmes, workshops, scientific seminars, and conferences for physicians. He also said that the organisation plans to expand nationwide awareness campaigns, conduct targeted screening among high-risk populations, introduce awareness programmes in educational institutions, and strengthen international collaboration. SAM Nazmul Hasan, associate professor in the Department of Liver Diseases at Bangladesh Medical University and Organising Secretary of the Hepatology Society, said the media has five key responsibilities: presenting science-based information; promoting preventive behaviour; encouraging people to undergo screening; highlighting fear, stigma and social discrimination; and playing a constructive role in shaping public health policy. Professor Abdullah Al Mahmud, head of the Department of Liver Diseases at Chattogram Medical College and Founding President of the Liver Care Society, said Bangladesh has been administering the hepatitis vaccine through the Expanded Programme on Immunisation (EPI) since 2003. He said everyone born before 2003 should undergo hepatitis screening. He added that surveys have found hepatitis infection rates ranging from 1 to 5 per cent among various occupational groups in Chattogram, including students, salon workers and hospital technicians. Professor Ruknuzzaman, chairman of the Department of Pediatric Gastroenterology at Bangladesh Medical University, said hepatitis A and hepatitis E spread primarily through contaminated food. He noted that street food is one of the major sources of these infections. He added that the hepatitis vaccine effectively protects people against liver cancer and therefore may also be regarded as a cancer-preventing vaccine. Professor Firoza Begum, president of the Obstetrical and Gynaecological Society of Bangladesh (OGSB), said hepatitis B and hepatitis C are among the leading causes of chronic diseases such as liver cirrhosis and liver cancer. She explained that these viruses spread from mother to child as well as from one infected person to another. Transmission from mother to newborn usually occurs during childbirth. She said newborns should receive the hepatitis vaccine within 12 hours of birth to prevent infection. Speaking about the history and significance of World Hepatitis Day, Professor Mohammad Golam Azam, general secretary of the Hepatology Society and Head of the Department of Liver and Gastroenterology at BIRDEM General Hospital, said fatty liver disease can lead to hepatitis. He added that alcohol consumption and certain tuberculosis medications may also cause hepatitis. In his closing remarks, Professor Mohammad Shahinul Alam, chairman of the Department of Hepatology at Bangladesh Medical University and president of the Hepatology Society, said, "The greatest obstacle to preventing or eliminating hepatitis is ignorance and a lack of awareness. Ninety per cent of infected people do not even know that they carry the virus. Around 8.5 million (85 lakh) people in Bangladesh are infected with hepatitis B, while another 1.5 million (15 lakh) are living with hepatitis C. However, treatment is available, and effective medicines exist." He added, "Preventing and controlling hepatitis requires social participation. Social participation means the involvement of all stakeholders, including physicians, the government, the state, the media, professional organisations and researchers." Firoz Choudhury, assistant editor of Prothom Alo, moderated the roundtable discussion

WHO Urges Africa To Break Barriers To Hepatitis Care - TVC News

News Brief
The World Health Organization (WHO) has called on African governments to break barriers to hepatitis care on World Hepatitis Day 2026. Dr. Mohamed Janabi, WHO Regional Director for Africa, emphasized that no one should die from a preventable, diagnosable, and treatable disease. Despite a 25% decline in new hepatitis B infections between 2015 and 2024, millions across Africa remain undiagnosed and untreated due to challenges like low awareness, delayed diagnosis, unequal access, and stigma. The WHO urges governments to strengthen national programs, expand services, and integrate hepatitis care into primary healthcare to meet the 2030 global elimination target.

By Kazeem Kasali July 20, 2026 1:32 PM GMT+1 The World Health Organization (WHO) has called on African governments to expand access to hepatitis prevention, testing and treatment, warning that millions of people across the continent remain undiagnosed and untreated despite the availability of effective interventions. In a message to mark World Hepatitis Day 2026, the WHO Regional Director for Africa, Dr Mohamed Janabi, said no one should die from a disease that can be prevented, diagnosed and, in many cases, treated. Observed under the theme, “Hepatitis: Let’s break it down,” this year’s campaign urges governments, health workers, partners and communities to remove barriers preventing people from accessing lifesaving hepatitis services. Although new hepatitis B infections in the WHO African Region have declined by 25 per cent between 2015 and 2024, Dr Janabi said viral hepatitis continues to claim too many lives because millions of people still lack timely diagnosis, treatment and care. He noted that highly effective hepatitis B vaccines, reliable diagnostic tools, curative treatment for hepatitis C and lifelong treatment for hepatitis B are already available. According to him, integrating these services into primary healthcare systems would save lives, reduce pressure on health services and accelerate progress towards universal health coverage. ADVERTISEMENT Dr Janabi commended the African Region for expanding hepatitis B birth-dose vaccination, advancing efforts to eliminate mother-to-child transmission of HIV, syphilis and hepatitis B, and pioneering hepatitis C elimination initiatives. He, however, stressed that these gains must be sustained and extended across all 47 WHO Member States. He also highlighted persistent challenges, including low awareness, delayed diagnosis, unequal access to testing and treatment, particularly in underserved communities, and the stigma associated with viral hepatitis. The WHO Regional Director urged Member States to strengthen national hepatitis programmes, expand vaccination, testing and treatment services, and place communities at the centre of elimination efforts. He also called on development partners to maintain technical and financial support to help countries meet the global target of eliminating viral hepatitis as a public health threat by 2030. Dr Janabi reaffirmed WHO’s commitment to supporting African countries in strengthening health systems, expanding equitable access to hepatitis services and preventing new infections across the continent. 罗氏推出全自动 cobas HDV 测试 - Select Science

Roche launches fully automated cobas HDV test - Select Science
新闻简报Roche has launched the cobas Hepatitis D Virus (HDV) test, the first fully automated, high-throughput molecular diagnostic assay for detecting and quantifying HDV RNA. Available in CE-mark countries, this test runs on widely implemented cobas 5800/6800/8800 systems, enabling standardized HDV testing with fast turnaround times. As hepatitis D affects nearly 12 million people worldwide and is the most severe form of viral hepatitis, this advancement supports clinicians in identifying patients with aggressive disease and monitoring their response to emerging treatments. The automation addresses previous inconsistencies in manual testing methods, potentially improving outcomes for patients with advanced liver disease where early intervention is critical.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Roche launches fully automated cobas HDV test - Select Science

News Brief
Roche has launched the cobas Hepatitis D Virus (HDV) test, the first fully automated, high-throughput molecular diagnostic assay for detecting and quantifying HDV RNA. Available in CE-mark countries, this test runs on widely implemented cobas 5800/6800/8800 systems, enabling standardized HDV testing with fast turnaround times. As hepatitis D affects nearly 12 million people worldwide and is the most severe form of viral hepatitis, this advancement supports clinicians in identifying patients with aggressive disease and monitoring their response to emerging treatments. The automation addresses previous inconsistencies in manual testing methods, potentially improving outcomes for patients with advanced liver disease where early intervention is critical.
Original source text (English)
Roche has introduced the cobas ® Hepatitis D Virus (HDV) test for use on the cobas ® 5800/6800/8800 systems in countries accepting the CE mark. This high-throughput, fully automated molecular diagnostic assay detects and quantifies HDV RNA, enabling clinicians to identify patients with the most aggressive form of viral hepatitis and monitor their response to newly available treatments. First fully automated, high-throughput HDV assay Until now, HDV testing has often relied on manual methods or laboratory-developed tests that can vary in performance and consistency. The cobas HDV test is the first high-throughput, fully automated HDV assay available on the market, designed to address the urgent need for a standardised, widely accessible diagnostic solution. By providing a consistent and scalable approach to HDV RNA detection and quantification, the cobas HDV test supports accurate diagnosis and ongoing monitoring of patients with hepatitis D. This is particularly important as specific HDV treatments become available in many markets, requiring precise viral load measurements to assess treatment response and guide clinical decisions. Seamless integration on cobas 5800/6800/8800 systems The cobas HDV test runs on the fully automated cobas 5800/6800/8800 systems, which are already widely implemented in clinical laboratories around the world. These molecular testing platforms enable laboratories to integrate HDV testing into existing workflows without major changes to infrastructure. Automation of the testing process allows laboratories to deliver accurate and reproducible results quickly and efficiently. Faster turnaround times support clinicians in making timely decisions for patients with advanced liver disease, where early intervention can be critical. Addressing the global burden of hepatitis D Hepatitis D affects nearly 12 million people worldwide and is recognized as the most severe form of viral hepatitis, progressing more rapidly to liver failure, cirrhosis, and liver cancer than other hepatitis viruses. HDV is a unique satellite virus that can only infect individuals who already have hepatitis B virus (HBV). It can present as a co-infection, where a person acquires HBV and HDV at the same time, or as a super-infection, where HDV infects someone with pre-existing chronic hepatitis B. Co-infection may account for around 1 in 5 cases of liver disease, while super-infection is particularly dangerous, with over 90% of cases becoming chronic and accelerating liver damage. Because HDV relies on HBV to replicate, vaccination against hepatitis B remains the most effective strategy to prevent hepatitis D infection. Supporting better outcomes in liver disease By expanding access to HDV testing on a widely used, automated platform, Roche aims to support hepatologists, infectious disease specialists, and clinical laboratories in improving the diagnosis and management of hepatitis D. The combination of standardized testing and emerging targeted therapies has the potential to significantly improve outcomes for people living with this severe form of viral hepatitis. “This is an important advancement of our commitment to improving outcomes for people living with liver disease,” said Matt Sause, CEO of Roche Diagnostics. “With the cobas HDV test, we are providing clinicians with a reliable tool to aid in the identification of infected individuals and to monitor their response to treatment.” Want the latest science news straight to your inbox? Become a SelectScience member for free today>> Frequently asked questions What is the Roche cobas HDV test and how does it improve Hepatitis D diagnosis? The Roche cobas ® Hepatitis D Virus (HDV) test is a high-throughput, fully automated molecular diagnostic assay for cobas ® 5800/6800/8800 systems in CE-mark countries. It detects and quantifies HDV RNA, providing standardized, reliable results that help clinicians identify patients with the most aggressive form of viral hepatitis and monitor their response to emerging HDV treatments. How does hepatitis D infection relate to hepatitis B and what are the main clinical risks? Hepatitis D is a satellite virus that only infects people with hepatitis B virus (HBV), either as a co-infection or super-infection. It is the most severe form of viral hepatitis, associated with faster progression to liver failure, cirrhosis, and hepatocellular carcinoma. Super-infection is especially dangerous, with over 90% of cases becoming chronic and rapidly accelerating liver damage. How do cobas 5800/6800/8800 systems support laboratories and clinicians managing Hepatitis D? The cobas ® 5800/6800/8800 systems are fully automated molecular testing platforms widely used in clinical laboratories. Integrating the cobas HDV test into these systems enables standardized, scalable HDV RNA testing with fast turnaround times. This supports hepatologists and infectious disease specialists in timely diagnosis, precise viral load monitoring, and treatment decision-making for patients with advanced liver disease caused by hepatitis D. Links Roche Tags Molecular Diagnostics Molecular diagnostics use an individual’s genetic code and gene expression to diagnose and monitor diseases. The technique is used increasingly in the field of infectious diseases and oncology, as well as areas such as coagulation, HLA typing and pharmacogenomics. Molecular diagnostics plays a pivotal role in personalized medicine. Hepatitis Infectious Disease Infectious disease diagnostics refers to the isolation and identification of causative agents, such as viruses, bacteria or fungi. Diagnostic tests include bacterial culture, microscopy, chemistry assays, molecular diagnostics and MALDI-TOF. Tests can be carried out in the laboratory or at the point-of-care

Roche has introduced the cobas Hepatitis D Virus (HDV) test, a high-throughput, fully automated molecular diagnostic assay for use on cobas 5800/6800/8800 systems. This is a confirmed product launch event.
Scale, impact and risk
Hepatitis D affects nearly 12 million people worldwide and is recognized as the most severe form of viral hepatitis. The test addresses the urgent need for standardized, widely accessible diagnostic solutions. No specific case counts or death rates mentioned in the evidence.
Response, status and uncertainty
The test supports accurate diagnosis and ongoing monitoring of patients with hepatitis D, particularly as specific HDV treatments become available. It enables laboratories to integrate HDV testing into existing workflows without major infrastructure changes. The source is a full original report body with no limitations mentioned.

无声无息的流行病:为什么印度现在必须对病毒性肝炎采取行动 - theweek.in

The silent epidemic: Why India must act on viral hepatitis now - theweek.in
新闻简报印度面临乙型和丙型病毒性肝炎的沉重负担,由于其无症状进展且缺乏公众紧迫感,通常被称为“无声流行病”。数百万人患有未确诊的感染,导致严重的肝脏损伤。虽然儿童 疫苗接种 已取得成功,但成人 疫苗接种 和常规筛查仍未得到充分利用。阿波罗医院的 Preetha Reddy 博士撰写的这篇文章强调了提高认识、成人 疫苗接种 计划以及医疗保健和工作场所综合筛查的必要性。早期发现和 治疗 是可能的,但耻辱和延迟 诊断 仍然存在。解决方案在于积极采取公共卫生措施,强调预防,以减轻未来的肝病负担。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

The silent epidemic: Why India must act on viral hepatitis now - theweek.in

News Brief
India faces a significant burden of viral hepatitis B and C, often called a 'silent epidemic' due to its asymptomatic progression and lack of public urgency. Millions live with undiagnosed infections, leading to severe liver damage. While childhood vaccination has been successful, adult vaccination and routine screening remain underutilized. The article, by Dr. Preetha Reddy of Apollo Hospitals, stresses the need for greater awareness, adult vaccination programs, and integrated screening in healthcare and workplaces. Early detection and treatment are possible, but stigma and delayed diagnosis persist. The solution lies in proactive public health measures, emphasizing prevention over treatment to reduce future liver disease burden.
Original source text (English)
SECTIONS journalism with a human touch Login My Profile Signed In as Profile Change Password Sign out Home Magazine Quick Read India World Sports Business Entertainment Reviews Columns Leisure More Hamburger Web Stories Videos Sci/Tech Health Defence Middle East Maritime Tourism Education Jobs & Career Search Subscribe Specials Middle East Defence Health Maritime × Search results for journalism with a human touch × News India World Sports Business Tourism Sci/Tech Entertainment Jobs & Career Magazine The Week Health Opinion Specials Health Defence Middle East Maritime Reviews Movies Books Gadgets Games Multimedia Videos Web Stories Quick Read Leisure Society Lifestyle Services About Us Privacy Policy Terms of Use Contact Us SIGN IN SUBSCRIBE Follow Us Columns Dr Preetha Reddy The silent epidemic: Why India must act on viral hepatitis now Greater public awareness, especially around adult vaccination and routine screening, is crucial for preventing and managing viral hepatitis Dr Preetha Reddy Published: July 19, 2026 08:21 AM IST Issue Date - July 26, 2026 12:08 PM IST 3 minute Read Share × Email Facebook Twitter WhatsApp LinkedIn Telegram Reddit 0 Comments Link Copied Every year, on July 28, World Hepatitis Day draws attention to one of the world’s most underestimated health challenges. The date marks the birthday of Nobel laureate Dr Baruch Blumberg, who discovered the hepatitis B virus and developed its first vaccine. Viral hepatitis rarely occupies public conversation with the urgency reserved for cancer, diabetes or heart disease, despite affecting millions of people and continuing to remain a major cause of liver cirrhosis and liver cancer globally. Presumably, a part of the reason lies in the nature of the disease itself. Hepatitis does not usually arrive dramatically. It progresses quietly, often unnoticed for years, allowing life to continue normally while silent inflammation gradually damages the liver. Fatigue is dismissed as stress. Mild digestive discomfort is ignored. In many cases, there are no symptoms at all until the disease has already advanced significantly. That silence is what makes hepatitis especially dangerous. India carries one of the world’s largest burdens of hepatitis B and hepatitis C. Millions are estimated to be living with these infections, many without diagnosis or treatment. Yet, unlike several other chronic illnesses, viral hepatitis presents a rare and important public health opportunity because prevention, early detection and effective treatment are all possible. Hepatitis B, for instance, is preventable through vaccination. India’s universal immunisation programme has helped protect millions of children over the years, representing one of the country’s important public health achievements. Yet, a large adult population remains outside that protective shield simply because routine vaccination was not widespread when they were growing up. Many continue to live vulnerable to infection without realising that protection is still available. Adult vaccination against hepatitis B remains far less discussed than it should be. Preventive health care conversations in India still tend to focus more on treatment than immunity, more on responding to illness than preventing it altogether. Expanding awareness around adult vaccination through workplaces, annual health evaluations, schools, community outreach and family medicine programmes could meaningfully reduce the country’s future burden of liver disease. The larger lesson extends beyond hepatitis. Public health systems become truly effective when prevention becomes part of everyday thinking rather than an afterthought reserved for moments of illness. This is why routine screening deserves far greater importance within preventive health care. High-risk individuals, health care workers, pregnant women and those with a family history of liver disease benefit significantly from early testing. Increasingly, hepatitis screening should also become part of regular health assessments and workplace wellness programmes, particularly in urban India where preventive health care participation is steadily increasing. Equally important is the need to remove stigma from conversations around liver disease. Viral hepatitis is not a reflection of personal failure. It is a medical condition that deserves openness, empathy and timely care. Silence and hesitation only delay the diagnosis. Across Apollo Hospitals, the consequences of delayed detection are visible all too often. Patients frequently seek medical attention only after liver disease has significantly advanced, despite years during which intervention might have altered outcomes entirely. This recognition has strengthened Apollo’s emphasis on preventive liver health through vaccination advocacy, integrated screening pathways, specialised hepatology programmes and coordinated multidisciplinary care focused on identifying disease earlier and improving long-term outcomes. The encouraging reality is that viral hepatitis is not a crisis without solutions. Few major diseases today offer such a powerful combination of prevention, early detection and effective treatment. The science already exists and the infrastructure continues to evolve. The remaining challenge is whether awareness can spread as effectively as the disease once did. Diseases that progress quietly often require the strongest public response. The future of liver health in India may depend less on discovering new medical breakthroughs and more on our willingness to act before the liver is finally forced to speak. Dr Preetha Reddy is Executive Vice Chairperson of Apollo Hospitals Enterprise Limited.
